# Changelog — November 2026

> Archived entries for November 2026, newest first. For recent work see [the live changelog](../changelog.md).

---

## 🐛 fix(a2a): tool names within 64 characters, never empty; card cache keyed by credential (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/a2a-tool-names`)

### What changed and why

- **A2A tool names over 64 characters.** The agent name and the skill id were each bounded at 64 characters, then joined, so a tool name reached 129. OpenAI, Anthropic and Gemini refuse a function name over 64, and one such name fails every request of the agent, not just the call to that tool: a 40-character configured agent name next to a 50-character peer skill id was enough, and a hostile peer card could do it on purpose. `A2AToolProviderManager` now makes every tool name in one place (`toolName`): each part sanitized, the whole cut to 64 with a short hash of the uncut name — the hash suffix `distinctNamePart` already used for skill ids, now shared — so two long ids that share a prefix stay two tools.
- **Empty A2A tool names.** Sanitizing keeps only `[a-z0-9_]`, so an agent named in a script other than Latin (`検索エージェント`) gave an empty name. `ToolSpecification` does not check the name, so the blank name reached the provider, which refuses it, and every request of the agent failed. With skills, the name lost its agent part, so two such peers collided on `lookup` and the second was skipped. A part that sanitizing empties now becomes `a2a_<hash of the original>` (`skill_<hash>` for a skill id).
- **A blank configured `name` now falls back to the card's name**, as an absent one always did. The Manager writes `""` once a typed name is cleared; that gave an empty or agent-less tool name before, and the shared `a2a` fallback after the change above, so a second such peer was skipped as a name collision.
- **Agent cards cached by URL alone.** A card fetched with one config's credential — possibly an authenticated card listing skills the peer shows only to that caller — was served for five minutes to every config naming the same URL with another credential or none, and its skills became their tools. The cache is now keyed on the URL plus a SHA-256 digest of the *unresolved* `apiKey`, as `McpToolProviderManager.cacheKey` does for MCP clients; configs naming one vault reference still share a card. An unauthenticated fetch keeps the bare URL as its key.
- The discovery branch for a credential or malformed-config failure now logs `A2A agent at … skipped: <reason>` instead of claiming every such failure was a credential problem (a card naming an endpoint on another origin lands there too).

### Design decisions

- **The 64-character cap is on the whole name, the per-part 64 stays.** The part bound exists for memory (an unbounded agent name was copied into every skill's tool name); the whole-name cap is what providers enforce. Each does its own job.
- **Fallback with a hash, not a bare `a2a`.** A bare fallback makes every non-Latin peer collide, and the second is silently skipped as a duplicate.
- **The digest helper is local, not a shared `RemoteCacheKey`.** Extracting one would edit `McpToolProviderManager`, outside this change; the codebase already keeps such SHA-256 helpers local to their class.

**Files:** [`A2AToolProviderManager.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/A2AToolProviderManager.java), [`A2AToolProviderManagerTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/A2AToolProviderManagerTest.java), [`a2a-protocol.md`](../a2a-protocol.md)

## fix(signing): private-key cache expires; unused provisioning and rotation methods removed (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/agent-signing-key-cache`)

### What changed and why

- **The loaded private key now expires after 60 s.** `AgentSigningService` kept a loaded agent
  signing key in a plain map for the life of the process. Only this node's own generate and delete
  paths evicted it, so a key replaced or removed in the vault any other way — on another node, or
  outside the service — was never picked up: the node kept signing with the old key, inter-agent
  self-verification against the new public key failed (the message went out unsigned), and audit
  entries carried signatures that no longer verified. The "no key" answer already expired after
  60 s; the "found" answer now does too. Both live in one Caffeine cache (`expireAfterWrite` 60 s,
  10,000 entries) on the existing test clock, replacing the two hand-rolled maps and the
  clear-everything size bound. The TTL constant is renamed `KEY_CACHE_TTL_NANOS` since it no longer
  covers only misses.
- **`generateKeyPair`, `generateKeyPairVersioned` and `rotateKey` are deleted**, with the
  eviction counter that guarded a race between a vault lookup and one of them. None had a
  production caller — `RestAgentStore.validateSecurityFlags` already said so — and
  [`architecture.md`](../architecture.md#cryptographic-agent-identity) claimed keys were
  "provisioned as part of agent creation", sending operators looking for a step that does not
  exist. The section now says what is true: nothing in EDDI provisions or rotates an agent signing
  key, the secrets API cannot write an `agent-signing-key:` name (its key-name pattern has no `:`),
  and an agent without a vault key signs nothing. `deleteKeyPair` and its version sweep stay. The
  deleted generators were the only code able to write such an entry, so making signing keys
  provisionable means bringing key generation back behind an admin operation.
- [`configuration-reference.md`](../configuration-reference.md) `eddi.audit.agent-signing-enabled`:
  the 60 s re-check now covers a replaced or removed key, not only a missing one.
- Comments that named the deleted methods now say what is true: `RestAgentStore.validateSecurityFlags`,
  `RestAgentStoreTest`, and `planning/manager-ui-handoff.md`, whose proposed "Generate Keypair" button
  now notes that it needs an admin endpoint first.
- **A vault outage now reaches signing after 60 s.** A loaded key used to outlive any vault failure;
  now, once it expires, a vault that cannot answer makes signing fail, so inter-agent messages go out
  unsigned (WARN) and audit entries carry no agent signature until the vault answers again. Nothing
  else changes: a vault error is never cached, and the vault shares the configuration database.

### Design decisions

- **Deletion over wiring an admin endpoint.** The alternative was a REST and MCP operation
  (`eddi-admin`) that calls the generators. That is a new capability surface — a route, an MCP
  tool, the operator prompt, docs — for a feature nobody has asked to provision yet; the methods
  were removed and later recovered once before (2026-05) without ever gaining a caller. The
  deletion is its own commit, so it can be reverted alone if the endpoint is wanted.
- **One cache for hits and misses** rather than a Caffeine cache beside the existing miss map:
  one TTL, one size bound, one eviction call, and `signIfKeyPresent`'s fast path is a single
  lookup.
- **Not `Cache.get(key, loader)`.** The vault read stays outside the cache's compute, as before,
  so a slow vault does not hold a map bin lock that blocks unrelated agents.

### Tests

`AgentSigningServiceTest`: a key replaced in the vault signs after the TTL and the old one no
longer does; inside the TTL the cached key signs with no second vault read; a key removed from the
vault elsewhere stops signing after the TTL; a key added for an agent just found keyless (unversioned
and rotated) signs after the TTL. The generator and rotation tests are gone; the remaining tests
provision keys by writing the vault entry directly, which is all the service reads.

**Files:** [`AgentSigningService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/AgentSigningService.java),
[`AgentSigningServiceTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/AgentSigningServiceTest.java),
[`architecture.md`](../architecture.md), [`configuration-reference.md`](../configuration-reference.md),
[`RestAgentStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/agents/rest/RestAgentStore.java),
[`manager-ui-handoff.md`](../../planning/manager-ui-handoff.md)

## 🐛 fix(apicalls): preRequest updates template data in place; final-attempt failure keys; fire-and-forget batch continues (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/api-call-executor-defects`)

### What changed and why

**`preRequest.propertyInstructions` no longer wipe the call's template data.**
`PrePostUtils.executePreRequestPropertyInstructions` ran the instructions and
then rebuilt the template data from conversation memory, returning a fresh map.
Everything the caller had put in the map was gone: the arguments an LLM tool
call merged in (`DELETE /items/{id}` went out as `DELETE /items/`), and in a
rule-triggered batch the `{responseObjectName}`, `…Error`, `…HttpCode` and
header values the call wrote, which the next call of the turn could no longer
template. An empty `propertyInstructions: []` list did the same. The method now
works like `runPostResponse`: the instructions update the caller's map in place
(each one already refreshed `{properties}` there), a plaintext a
`scope: "secret"` instruction vaulted is scrubbed from it, and the same map is
returned. `McpCallsTask`, which assigns the return value the same way, is fixed
by the same change; `LlmTask` ignored the return value, so it kept its map all
along and now also gets the scrub. `PrePostUtils` no longer needs `IMemoryItemConverter`; the
constructor parameter is gone. The HITL pinning predicate
(`canExecuteDivergeFromResolve`) is unchanged — a call with pre-request
instructions stays unpinnable — and its Javadoc now gives the real reason.

**A retried call's failure keys come from its final attempt.** On a non-2xx
response with `saveResponse`, every attempt wrote `{name}Error` and
`{name}HttpCode` to memory and to the template data, and nothing removed them:
a `503` followed by a successful retry left `weatherHttpCode=503` and the 503
body next to a good `weather`, where `postResponse`, output templates and the
next call of the turn all saw a failure. A `503` with a body followed by a
`500` with a blank one paired the first body with the second code. Now the
template data holds the current attempt's failure keys only (they are removed
before the next attempt, so a `responseValuePathMatchers` entry on
`{name}Error` still judges the response it decides about), and memory records
the final attempt's failure once, after the loop — as the `forEach` path does.
A matcher that reads the same keys through memory
(`memory.current.httpCalls.{name}HttpCode`) used to see each attempt because
every attempt was written to the live step output; the retry decision now gets
a copy of the template data with the attempt's failure keys under that path, so
it still sees the attempt it decides about while memory stays final-only.

**One failed request of a fire-and-forget batch no longer drops the rest.**
A `fireAndForget` call with `preRequest.batchRequests` sends its requests as
one background task. With `executeCallsSequentially`, a request that threw (a
timeout, a connection reset) ended that task, so every later request was never
sent; the only trace was a generic runtime error naming neither the call nor
the item. In the concurrent mode the same happened when a dispatch threw
synchronously. Each request is now sent on its own: a failure is logged with
the call name, its position (`request 2 of 5`) and a redacted reason, the rest
of the batch still goes out, and a summary line counts the failures. An
interrupted batch thread stops sending and says how many were left.

### Behaviour changes

- A `preRequest` property instruction with a `toObjectPath` now writes into the
  template data the request renders from; the rebuild used to discard it.
- Nothing a template read through the rebuilt map is lost: `{properties.x}` is
  refreshed in place by every instruction, and `{memory.current…}` is a live view
  of the current step, which already shows the property just set (under
  `memory.current.properties`) without a rebuild.
- A retry request can no longer template the previous attempt's
  `{name}HttpCode` / `{name}Error`. That was undocumented, and it is the
  divergence between the previewed and the sent request that the HITL pinning
  guard warns about.
- Memory gets one `{name}Error` / `{name}HttpCode` entry per call instead of one
  per failed attempt, written after the response headers rather than before.

**Files:** [`PrePostUtils.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/apicalls/impl/PrePostUtils.java),
[`ApiCallExecutor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/apicalls/impl/ApiCallExecutor.java),
[`httpcalls.md`](../httpcalls.md) (`executeCallsSequentially` and `retryApiCallInstruction.maxRetries` rows);
tests `PrePostUtilsTest`, `PrePostUtilsDeepBranchTest`, `PrePostUtilsSecretScopeTest`,
`ApiCallExecutorTest`, `ApiCallExecutorPreRequestTest` (new), `ApiCallExecutorFireAndForgetBatchTest` (new),
and the constructor call in the other `PrePostUtils` users.

## 🐛 fix(channels): the channelId uniqueness check fails closed like the name check (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/channel-id-uniqueness`)

### What changed and why

Two channel integrations must not bind the same `channelType:channelId`: the
router keys its routing map on it, so a second one silently takes over the
first one's channel. `validateUniqueChannelId` enforced that, but it swallowed
every failure: an entry it could not read was skipped at debug level, and a scan
that could not run at all logged "allowing save". One store timeout while
reading the integration that held `C12345` was enough to store a second
integration bound to `C12345`. The name check right below it already failed
closed (503) and skipped only a missing or undeserialisable document.

The three near-identical descriptor scans (channelId check, name check, and the
copy-name collector of `duplicateChannel`) are now one private walker,
`forEachOtherIntegration`, with the name check's error policy:

- a descriptor whose integration no longer exists is skipped;
- a stored document that no longer deserialises is skipped, so one corrupt
  entry cannot block every channel write;
- anything else (descriptors cannot be listed, an entry cannot be read right
  now) refuses the save with **503**, "Could not verify that the channel
  integration channelId is unique. Please retry."

### Behaviour changes

- Creating or updating an integration with a `channelId` answers 503 instead of
  saving when the uniqueness scan hits a transient store failure. Retrying
  succeeds once the store answers.
- Duplicating an integration answers 503 one step earlier (while picking the
  copy's name) on the same failure; the name check that followed already
  answered 503 for it.

### Known limit

The scan and its lock serialise saves on one node only; two nodes saving the
same `channelId` at the same moment can still both pass. Recorded in the
walker's Javadoc.

**Files:** [`RestChannelIntegrationStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/channels/rest/RestChannelIntegrationStore.java),
[`RestChannelIntegrationStoreCrudTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/channels/rest/RestChannelIntegrationStoreCrudTest.java),
[`slack-integration.md`](../slack-integration.md)

## 🐛 fix(deployment): cluster wait never enters the sweep; tracking derived from the registry (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/deployment-tracking`)

### What changed and why

Two defects in `AgentDeploymentManagement`, both in cluster mode.

- **A request waited behind the whole deployment sweep.** When a request reached a node
  before that node had deployed an agent another node had just deployed,
  `awaitClusterDeployment` ran the full sweep under its lock before it started its
  15 s wait. The request queued behind any running sweep, deployed every other agent the
  node was missing before its own, and a burst of requests ran one full sweep each. A
  version whose deployment had failed on the node cost every request the full 15 s,
  because it never became ready. The wait now starts its clock first, reads the
  deployment records and this node's registry once, and submits a deployment of just the
  missing versions of the requested agent to the runtime executor. Requests that arrive
  while that deployment is queued or running do not submit it again. A version in `ERROR`
  on this node is neither retried nor waited for, since the sweep owns retries and their
  backoff. While the startup migrations run or are deferred, nothing is deployed on
  demand, as before.
- **A version this node undeployed itself stayed "tracked".** The sweep kept a private
  list of the deployments it had confirmed READY and skipped them from then on. Only an
  undeploy that arrived as an event from another node removed an entry. A REST undeploy on
  this node, the daily retirement, the delete cascade and sub-agent teardown left the entry
  in place. When the same version was deployed again by a request on another node, this
  node's sweep found it tracked and never deployed it, and that node's share of the
  traffic waited 15 s and was answered as not ready until a restart. The sweep now starts
  by dropping every tracked row this node's registry does not hold READY. A registry that
  cannot be read drops nothing. The tracked set is a concurrent set, so an undeploy event
  no longer takes the sweep lock: one undeploy arriving during a long sweep used to stall
  every later cluster event on the node, cache invalidations included. The sweep reads
  the registry before the deployment records, so an undeploy (registry first, record
  second) that lands while the sweep runs is not taken for a deployment that went
  missing.
- **An undeploy-only teardown now marks the deployment record undeployed.**
  `teardown_agent` without `delete`, and the end-of-discussion cleanup when it only
  undeploys (`lifecyclePolicy: UNDEPLOY_ONLY`, or an agent whose origin cannot be
  verified), removed the sub-agent from this node's registry and left its record saying
  deployed. Once tracking follows the registry, the next sweep would deploy it again
  within about ten seconds; before, it came back at the next restart, and other nodes in
  a cluster never undeployed it. Both paths now flip the agent's `production` records to
  undeployed, as a REST undeploy does, so every node's sweep undeploys it too.

### Design decisions

- Tracking is derived from `IAgentFactory.getAllDeployedAgents` on every sweep instead of
  hooking every local undeploy path. The registry is the fact; the list was a second copy
  of it, the same lesson `AgentFactory` records for its `eddi_agents_deployed` gauge.
- The on-demand path reads the registry with `getAllDeployedAgents`, not `getAgent`, which
  blocks up to 60 s on a deployment in progress.
- Rejected: running `checkDeployments()` asynchronously from the request. It would still
  deploy every other missing agent before this one and still queue one sweep per request.

**Files:** [`AgentDeploymentManagement.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagement.java),
[`IAgentDeploymentManagement.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/IAgentDeploymentManagement.java),
[`AgentDeploymentManagementClusterTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagementClusterTest.java),
[`AgentDeploymentManagementBranchTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagementBranchTest.java),
[`GroupLifecycleOps.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/internal/groups/GroupLifecycleOps.java),
[`TeardownAgentTool.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/tools/TeardownAgentTool.java),
[`clustering.md`](../clustering.md),
[`group-conversations.md`](../group-conversations.md)

## 🐛 fix(email): pseudonymised audit, constructor-injected collaborators, bounded reply cache, one retry policy (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/email-channel`)

### What changed and why

- **Audit entries name a mail address only by its keyed pseudonym.** A refused inbound mail (`email.inbound`) wrote
  `senderHash` and a sent, failed or bounced reply (`email.reply`) wrote `recipientHash`: an unkeyed SHA-256 next to
  the address's domain, in a write-once ledger erasure cannot reach. Anyone holding a list of candidate addresses at
  that domain could hash them and tell whose mail it was. Both entries now carry `senderPseudonym` /
  `recipientPseudonym` from `GdprPseudonymizer` — the keyed token HITL notifications (`email.notify`) already wrote
  under that name — and the bounce-suppression log line names the address the same way. The suppression list keeps
  its SHA-256: it is a lookup key, not audit output. Entries written before this change keep their `*Hash` field. The
  token is keyed with the audit ledger's key, or salted per deployment; only a deployment with neither falls back to
  an unsalted hash.
- **`EmailInboundProcessor` takes every collaborator through its constructor.** The reply sender and the audit ledger
  were field-injected and documented as "null in tests, and then no reply is ever attempted"; production code branched
  on `replies != null` three times, so a construction site that forgot them silently turned conversation-mode replies,
  group replies and bounce handling off. They are final constructor parameters now, with the pseudonymizer, and the
  null branches are gone; whether an integration replies is still `settings.replies()`.
- **The group-reply cache is bounded without forgetting everything.** A group reply's rendered request lives in the
  node's memory for its retries. The map was cleared wholesale once it reached 1,000 entries, and entries left only
  when the row ended on this node, so a row another node's sweep finished stayed forever; in a cluster the map filled
  until a clear dropped every reply still mid-retry, and each of those ended `FAILED (CONTENT_UNAVAILABLE)`. It is a
  Caffeine cache now: past 1,000 one entry is evicted, and an entry expires once its row cannot be retrying any more
  (`(lease + max backoff) × max-attempts`).
- **One retry policy for the email ledger's outbound rows.** The capped exponential backoff was copied into the HITL
  notifier, the reply sender and the mailbox poller, and the "fail, or reschedule and count a retry" branch four times;
  the copies had drifted — an unexpected (non-SMTP) reply failure was rescheduled but never counted in
  `eddi.email.outbound{kind=reply,outcome=retry}`. `LedgerRetry` holds the backoff and that branch; the three classes
  use it, and the reply sender now counts that retry.

### Design decisions

- `LedgerRetry` is a small instance (ledger, max attempts, base) rather than static helpers with nine parameters: it
  also owns the `max-attempts` / `retry-backoff` defaults both senders normalised separately. The send flows around it
  (retention, the pending gauge, content rebuilding) differ and stay in their classes — one shared worker would be a
  template method over two unlike flows.
- The cache's expiry is per write, not per access: the bound is "the longest a row can stay retryable", which is a
  property of the row's age, and a direct executor makes size eviction happen on the writing thread.

**Files:** [`LedgerRetry.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/ledger/LedgerRetry.java),
[`EmailInboundProcessor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/EmailInboundProcessor.java),
[`EmailReplySender.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/reply/EmailReplySender.java),
[`HitlEmailNotifier.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/notify/HitlEmailNotifier.java),
[`EmailMailboxPoller.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/EmailMailboxPoller.java),
[`email-integration.md`](../email-integration.md)

### What's next

- `EmailInboundSweeper` still field-injects its `EmailReplySender` the same way; it was outside this change.

## 🐛 fix: findings from the documentation and comment reviews — dead config fields, `lookupIfKnown`, vault messages, counterweight metrics, classifier tool-call layout (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/docs-review-findings`)

### What changed and why

A documentation review turned up five places where the code did not do what its config surface,
Javadoc or comments said. Each is fixed test-first.

**1. `dream.batchSize` / `dream.maxUsersPerRun` — removed from the model, still accepted on input.**
Nothing ever read them. They describe a sweep over all users in batches, which was never built: a
dream fire consolidates exactly the one user its schedule names, so there is no batch to size and
no per-run user count to cap. Wiring them up would mean inventing that sweep. They are no longer
fields of `DreamConfig`; `@JsonIgnoreProperties({"batchSize", "maxUsersPerRun"})` keeps a stored
agent or an imported ZIP that still carries them deserializing, including through the strict mapper
that REST writes use, and the next save drops them. The Manager's agent editor offered a "Batch
Size" input that wrote to the dead field; it is gone, along with the two fields in its
`DreamConfig` type. The i18n key stays in the locale files (the i18n check flags orphans and
missing keys, not unused ones).

**2. `ScheduleConfiguration.tenantId` / `allowSelfScheduling` — deprecated, kept.** Neither has
ever been read by a fire, and neither the Javadoc nor `scheduling.md` promised a behaviour — they
only described the PUT carry-over. `tenantId` has no tenant model to plug into on the fire path,
and self-booking is already governed by the LLM task whitelisting `schedule_followup`, so a second
switch on an existing schedule would only be a second place to look. Unlike the Dream fields these
are real columns (PostgreSQL) and indexed fields (MongoDB) with explicit PUT carry-over semantics,
so removing them means schema churn for no gain. They are marked `@Deprecated` on the field with
Javadoc on the accessors saying they are stored and not read; the field table in `scheduling.md`
now lists both as deprecated and not read.

**3. `lookupIfKnown` threw `ClassCastException` on a JSON boolean.**
`IParserExtensionProvider.extractLookupIfKnownParam` cast the value to `String`, so
`"lookupIfKnown": true` failed the parser workflow. It now accepts a `Boolean` as is and reads
anything else by its trimmed string form (`"true"`, case-insensitive). Both providers that read it,
Damerau-Levenshtein and phonetic, now advertise it in their `ExtensionDescriptor`
(`BOOLEAN`, optional, default `false`); the phonetic provider had no descriptor entries at all.
`semantic-parser.md` documents the key.

**4. Helm `values.yaml` named the wrong chart version.** It said `nats.buildProfileImage` stopped
being read "from chart < 2.5"; the release that made it so (775edae28) moved the chart from 2.6.0 to
2.7.0, which is what `NOTES.txt` says. Fixed to 2.7.0, and a test now asserts the two copies agree.
Chart `version` 2.14.0 → 2.14.1 (comment-only change, so a patch) with `EXPECTED_CHART_VERSION`.

**5. `GET /secretstore/secrets/health` reported the ArC client proxy.** `RestSecretStore` took
`getClass().getSimpleName()` of the injected `@ApplicationScoped` `ISecretProvider`, which under
Quarkus is `VaultSecretProvider_ClientProxy`, not the documented `VaultSecretProvider` — the
released 6.7.0 image answers exactly that. It now
unwraps the proxy with `ClientProxy.unwrap` before naming the class.

### From the Java comment review

**6. `LifecycleInterruptedException(message, cause)` dropped the cause.** It called `super(message)`;
now `super(message, e)`. `RetryConfiguration`'s interrupted back-off is the caller, so the interrupt
now stays on the stack trace.

**7. `ClusterConfig.redactUserInfo` — `\s` kept, made explicit, guarded.** In a Java string `"\s"`
is the space escape, so the class `[^/@\s]` excluded only spaces. The suggested fix, the regex
`\\s`, would have been a regression: a tab or newline *inside* the userinfo would then end the
match before the `@`, and the credentials would be logged in clear. The class is now spelled with a
literal space (same behaviour, no ambiguity), and a test pins that a tab or newline in the userinfo
is still redacted.

**8. `ContractedWordNormalizer` mapped "won't" to "would not".** Now "will not"; the existing test
asserted the wrong expansion and is corrected. The other entries are right (the `'s` forms are
ambiguous between "is" and "has"; "is" is kept).

**9. `RemoteApiResourceSource.resolveLatestAgentVersion` threw a bare `RuntimeException`** for an
agent the remote does not list. It is now a `RemoteReadException` like every other source-read
failure. The only caller (`readAgent`) already wrapped it in one, so the HTTP status (502) was
right; the cause type and message were the inconsistency.

**10. The vault's DEK-failure recovery hint named `POST …/reset` without `?confirm=true`,** which
that endpoint refuses with 400. All three hints (two exception messages and the master-key adoption
warning) now carry it.

**11. `AgentStoreClientLibrary` logged under `AgentFactory`'s category.** Now its own.

**12. `CounterweightService` counted an unknown `level` as `normal`.** An unknown level resolves no
preset, and the early "no injection" return ran before the switch whose `default` arm warns and
counts `unknown`, so that arm was unreachable and a typo such as `"stirct"` looked like a healthy
normal activation. The level is classified first: unknown warns and counts `unknown` (only), and
`normal` with custom instructions — which also fell into the `default` arm — counts `normal`.

**13. `ClassifierGuardrail` without a `promptTemplate` sent tool-call arguments as the user's
message.** `buildMessages` chose the conversation layout on `phase == OUTPUT`, while
`GuardrailModelClient.templateData` uses `phase != INPUT`. With a template, tool-call arguments fill
`{output}` (as `tool-call-guardrails.md` documents); without one they went out as a plain user
message, so the classifier judged the model's own tool call as if the user had typed it. Both now
treat every non-input phase as model output: the user's last message, then the arguments as the
assistant's turn.

**14. The vault-disabled banner and the compliance check misstated audit signing.** The banner said
audit entries "will NOT have HMAC integrity signatures", but `eddi.audit.hmac-key` signs the ledger
without a master key. It now says entries are signed only if `EDDI_AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` is set. The
compliance check's logic was already right (it warns only when both keys are missing); its banner
and its startup failure now say "neither … nor" and list both ways to enable signing.

**Review follow-ups.** The four `ScheduleConfiguration` accessors for `tenantId` and `allowSelfScheduling` are now `@Deprecated(since = "6.8.0")` too, not just the fields. `CounterweightService.resolveInjection` lowercased with the default locale, so under a Turkish locale `"STRICT"` became `"strıct"` and the preset was silently skipped; it now uses `Locale.ROOT` on the already-normalised level (new test under `tr-TR`). The vault-disabled banner names the property `eddi.audit.hmac-key` and gives `EDDI_AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` as the env example.

**Checked and left alone:**

- `PostgresHealthCheck` does not mark readiness DOWN on the MongoDB default. Probed against the
  released `labsai/eddi:6.7.0` image: on MongoDB and on PostgreSQL alike, `/q/health/ready` lists
  no "PostgreSQL connection" check at all — the `@DefaultBean` is never selected, since other
  `HealthCheck` beans exist. PostgreSQL readiness is covered by Agroal's own "Database connections
  health check", which is present and UP on both. So the suspected failure does not happen; the
  class is dead code, left for a follow-up rather than changed here.
- `ScheduleFireExecutor.creatorUseCheck` and lesson-consolidation schedules: such a schedule cannot
  carry both an `agentId` and a creator snapshot. `RestGroupLessons` sets neither, REST create and
  ZIP import refuse a body with the lesson marker (`rejectLessonConsolidationBody`), and a PUT
  carries over the stored null `creatorTeams`, which skips the check. Not reachable.

### Tests

New: `LookupIfKnownConfigTest`; `RestSecretStoreTest` (proxied and unproxied provider name);
`AgentConfigurationTest` (legacy Dream fields deserialize strictly and are not written back);
`DeploymentManifestsTest#buildProfileImageRemovalVersionAgrees`; `LifecycleInterruptedExceptionTest`;
`JetStreamDeadLetterStoreTest` (tab/newline userinfo); `RemoteApiResourceSourceMissedBranchTest`
(unlisted agent); `AgentStoreClientLibraryLoggerTest`; `CounterweightServiceTest` (unknown level,
normal with custom instructions); `ClassifierGuardrailTest` (tool-call phase, no template);
`VaultStartupBannerTest`; assertions added to `ComplianceStartupChecksTest`, `VaultKeySafetyTest`,
`VaultSecretProviderBranchTest` and `ContractedWordNormalizerTest`. Each failed with its fix
reverted, except the `redactUserInfo` guard, which passes on the old code by design and fails
against the suggested `\\s` change.

## fix(groups): atomic budget cap sets and exact outcome caps on both stores (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/group-atomic-stores`)

### What changed and why

- **Setting a team's monthly cap is a compare-and-set.** `TeamBudgetService.setCurrentMonthCap` read the month, computed
  `delta = capUsd - cap` and `$inc`'d the adjustment by it. Two sets racing on a cap of 50 — to 100 and to 80, say an
  administrator and an approved `TeamBudgetProposalTool` proposal — stored 50 + 50 + 30 = 130, a cap nobody asked for,
  while the audit said 50 → 100 and 50 → 80. `ITeamBudgetStore` gains `setAdjustment(expected, new, …)` with a new
  `AdjustOutcome.CONFLICT`: MongoDB applies it as one `findOneAndUpdate` whose filter carries the expected adjustment and
  the "cap stays at or above the spend" predicate, PostgreSQL under the row lock it already took for transfers. The
  service re-reads and retries a conflict up to three times (the later set wins, and its audit entry names the cap it
  actually replaced), then answers 503. Transfers keep `applyAdjustment`: a delta is the right meaning there.
- **The per-subject outcome cap holds under concurrent posts.** `OutcomeEventService.record` counted the subject and
  inserted in two steps, so N concurrent posts at `maxPerSubject - 1` all stored. `IOutcomeEventStore.insertIfAbsent`
  becomes `insert(event, maxPerSubject)` answering `INSERTED` / `DUPLICATE` / `FULL` (a duplicate wins over full, so a
  retried webhook on a full subject still gets 200). PostgreSQL counts and inserts in one transaction behind a
  per-subject `pg_advisory_xact_lock`, as `PostgresAttachmentStore` does. MongoDB has no transactions in EDDI, so each
  event claims the lowest free `slot` of its subject under a new unique partial index
  `idx_outcome_subject_slot (groupId, subjectId, slot)`: two writers that read the same subject pick the same slot and
  only one stores. The service's own count, now redundant, is gone.
- **Dead code removed:** `OutcomeEventService.retroDigestProvider()` (the RETRO digest is wired through
  `retroDigest` directly), `SharedTaskList.findTasksForAgent` (only its own two tests called it) and
  `IOutcomeEventStore.countBySubject` with both store implementations (its one caller was the service's count).

### Design decisions

- **Slots rather than insert-then-check on MongoDB.** Inserting and then deleting the event if it is not among the
  subject's oldest `maxPerSubject` over-admits whenever an event that sorts earlier is inserted after a later one's
  check — under eight racing writers, often. The slot index makes the cap exact for a constant `maxPerSubject`; after a
  lowered cap, or for rows written before this change (none in a release: outcomes are new in 6.8.0), the up-front
  count still refuses sequentially.
- **Three attempts, then 503** for a set that keeps losing: a set that conflicts has a fresh snapshot to retry with,
  and three consecutive losses mean sustained contention an administrator should retry, not a loop to spin in.

**Files:** [`ITeamBudgetStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/budget/ITeamBudgetStore.java),
[`MongoTeamBudgetStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/budget/MongoTeamBudgetStore.java),
[`PostgresTeamBudgetStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/budget/PostgresTeamBudgetStore.java),
[`TeamBudgetService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/budget/TeamBudgetService.java),
[`IOutcomeEventStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/outcomes/IOutcomeEventStore.java),
[`OutcomeEventService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/outcomes/OutcomeEventService.java),
[`MongoOutcomeEventStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/groups/outcomes/mongo/MongoOutcomeEventStore.java),
[`PostgresOutcomeEventStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/postgres/PostgresOutcomeEventStore.java),
[`SharedTaskList.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/groups/model/SharedTaskList.java);

## 🐛 fix(mcp): schedule tools go through the REST schedule store under the caller's identity (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/mcp-schedule-tools-authz`)

### What changed and why

The six MCP schedule tools (`create_schedule`, `list_schedules`, `read_schedule`,
`delete_schedule`, `fire_schedule_now`, `retry_failed_schedule`) addressed `IScheduleStore`,
`ScheduleFireExecutor` and `SchedulePollerService` directly and kept their own copy of the
parts of `RestScheduleStore` someone had remembered to copy. The copy had drifted:

- `create_schedule` ran none of REST's validation, so a conversation strategy such as
  `sometimes` and a `userId` in the agent-test namespace were stored, and an unknown time
  zone or a cron that never matches answered an internal error instead of the reason.
- It never stamped `createdBy`, `creatorTeams` or `creatorAdmin`. With `createdBy` null,
  `ScheduleFireExecutor` skips the fire-time re-check of the creator's access to the agent
  for every fire of that schedule.
- `fire_schedule_now` refused only HITL timeouts and continuations. REST also requires
  `EDIT` on the knowledge base, integration or group an ingestion, email-poll, team-cadence
  or lesson-consolidation schedule drives, refuses a marker that names none (400), and runs
  its owner and manage checks.

The tools are admin-only, so this was drift rather than a bypass reachable today. They are
now thin adapters over the in-process `IRestScheduleStore` bean, which reads the request's
`SecurityIdentity` (the MCP caller), the pattern `McpAlertTools` and `McpRatingTools`
already use. `McpAdminTools` loses its three schedule dependencies, the
`eddi.schedule.min-interval-seconds` copy and the duplicated create and fire logic.

`McpToolUtils.responseJson` now keeps a plain-text response body as the message (the
schedule resource answers its refusals as text, which used to degrade to "The request
failed (HTTP 409)") and maps 504 to `errorCode: TIMEOUT`.

### Behaviour changes

- Refusals carry REST's text and code: `invalid_schedule` for a schedule REST would not
  create, `CONFLICT`/`FORBIDDEN`/`BAD_REQUEST`/`TIMEOUT` on a manual fire,
  `SYSTEM_SCHEDULE` on deleting the server's own schedule.
- An unset `timeZone` on `create_schedule` takes `eddi.schedule.default-timezone`; the tool
  argument no longer defaults to `UTC` itself.
- A `heartbeatIntervalSeconds` makes a schedule a heartbeat even with `triggerType: CRON`,
  as over REST. An unknown `triggerType` is refused with `invalid_schedule`.
- `list_schedules` is paged: new `offset` (default 0) and `limit` (default 100, max 500)
  arguments. It fetches one row more than the page, so `truncated: true` (with `nextOffset`)
  is reported only when more schedules really exist; an exactly-full last page is not truncated. `cronDescription` now also describes heartbeats.
- `delete_schedule` on an id that does not exist succeeds, as `DELETE` does over REST.

**Files:** [`McpAdminTools.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpAdminTools.java),
[`McpToolUtils.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpToolUtils.java),
[`McpRoles.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpRoles.java),
[`McpScheduleParityTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/schedule/rest/McpScheduleParityTest.java),
[`McpScheduleToolsTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpScheduleToolsTest.java),
[`mcp-server.md`](../mcp-server.md#schedule-management-tools-6)

### Next

Widening the schedule tools from admin-only to REST's admin/editor tier is now a decision
about the tier alone, since REST's owner rules apply to the MCP caller. Left for a
separate change.

## 🐛 fix(migration): migrated Slack channels get descriptors; the legacy migration loses its fake running guard (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/migration-descriptors`)

### What changed and why

- **Migrated channels were invisible.** [`ChannelConnectorMigration`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/migration/ChannelConnectorMigration.java)
  turned the deprecated `channels` list on an agent into channel integrations with `channelStore.create` and wrote no
  descriptor. Everything that finds a channel integration lists `ai.labs.channel` descriptors — the Slack router, the
  REST and MCP listings, and the migration's own duplicate check — and `DocumentDescriptorFilter` runs only for HTTP
  responses. So the router kept routing through the legacy connector, the integrations appeared nowhere, and a re-run
  after one failed channel created every channel again. Each created channel now gets its descriptor, with the access
  index built (`DescriptorAccess.rebuildIndex`), named like the config, and owned and filed like the agent it routes
  to by default. That agent's visibility is copied only when it is `private`; anything wider becomes `space`, since a
  channel holds the bot's credentials. A descriptor that cannot be written fails that channel (no completion flag) and
  removes the document again, so the retry does not duplicate it. An agent with no descriptor leaves its channel
  unowned, as the agent is; an agent descriptor that cannot be *read* aborts the run instead (no completion flag), since
  an unowned channel is open to everyone under the default legacy policy and an unknown owner is not an absent one.
- **A failed duplicate check no longer creates duplicates.** When the channel listing itself failed, the run went on
  "but may create duplicates". It now aborts and retries on the next start.
- **Back-fill.** The completion flag is renamed to `channel-connector-migration-v2-complete`, so a deployment that
  finished the first run migrates once more and gets visible integrations; Slack then routes through them. The
  documents the first run created stay in the database unreferenced: no API lists or reads them. Noted in
  [Upgrading from 6.5](../upgrading-from-6.5.md#slack-channels-still-declared-on-an-agent).
- **The document migrations run on one node at a time.** [`AgentDeploymentManagement.runDocumentMigrations`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/AgentDeploymentManagement.java)
  (Qute, channel connector, workspace access index) had no cluster-wide exclusion: each migration reads its flag,
  works, then writes the flag, so replicas starting together all ran the pass. The in-place rewrites converge, but the
  channel migration *creates* a configuration under a fresh id, and its duplicate check cannot see what another node
  is creating at that moment, so each channel was migrated once per node — now visible ones, and the renamed flag
  makes every existing cluster run it again on upgrade. The pass now holds the cluster lease `migration.documents`
  (the existing `IConversationLeaseManager`; NATS KV in cluster mode, granted at once on a single node). A waiting
  node gets it when the holder is done and finds every flag set; a crashed holder loses it after the lease TTL. A node
  still without it after 15 minutes, or refused it while NATS is unreachable under `degraded.turns=reject`, skips the
  pass with a WARN and starts as before; what is left is retried on the next start.
- **`MigrationManager` drops `isCurrentlyRunning`.** [`startMigrationIfFirstTimeRun`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/configs/migration/MigrationManager.java)
  is `synchronized`, so the flag could only ever be seen by a call from inside its own callback — which it then
  skipped without running that caller's callback. Nothing calls back in today; the two tests named "concurrent calls
  guarded" made two sequential calls, and are renamed to say so.
- **`V6RenameMigration` counts a failed agent rewrite.** The `packages` → `workflows` pass over `agents` called
  `replaceOne` bare and counted the document migrated whatever came back; it now goes through `saveDocument`, like the
  history pass beside it, so a replace that matched nothing (or threw) keeps the migration incomplete and it retries.

### Tests

`ChannelConnectorMigrationTest`: the descriptor's resource URI, name, owner, space, narrowed visibility and index; a
private agent's channel stays private; an agent without a descriptor gives an unowned channel, and an unreadable one
aborts the run; a re-run after a partial failure creates only the failed channel (stores that
remember the first run's writes); a descriptor write failure removes the document and leaves the flag unset; a listing
failure creates nothing; the first run's flag does not skip the second. `MigrationManagerTest`: a call from inside the
completion callback runs its own callback. `V6RenameMigrationTest`: an agent rewrite that matched nothing leaves the
completion log unwritten, and one that matched writes it. `AgentDeploymentManagementMigrationLeaseTest`: the lease
acquired runs the three migrations inside it and releases it; held elsewhere, refused while degraded, or unanswered
past the wait, the pass is skipped and startup still deploys; a migration that throws still releases the lease.

## 🐛 fix(rules): refuse unsupported deployment tags, quick-reply templating, property and dependency fixes (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/rules-output-property`)

### What changed and why

- **Templated quick replies are now shown templated.** The output task stores each quick reply twice: in the `quickReplies:<action>` data entry and in the conversation output the client receives. `OutputTemplateTask` rendered only the data entry, so the client showed `Call {properties.agentName}` while the next turn's dictionary, built from the data entry, held `Call Ava`. A click on the button the user saw matched nothing. The rendered quick replies now replace the raw ones in the conversation output too, as rendered output texts already did. Quick replies sent in `context` are still shown as they arrived.
- **`deploymentContext` refuses `tagMatches`.** The condition read agent tags from an `agent:tags` memory key that nothing writes, and `AgentConfiguration` has no tags field, so a rule with `tagMatches` never fired. That included the Javadoc's own example, a safety rule meant to make production agents cautious. A non-blank `tagMatches` is now refused when the ruleset is read, with an error that names the rule and points to `contextmatcher`. The Manager's default for a new `deploymentContext` condition no longer carries the field. **Behaviour change:** a stored ruleset that uses `tagMatches` now fails to save or deploy until the field is removed. Such a rule never matched, so removing the field changes nothing at runtime.
- **A property instruction runs once per turn.** `PropertySetterTask` collected and applied instructions separately for each of the turn's actions. An instruction under `"*"`, or under two of the turn's actions, therefore ran once per action. For `scope: secret`, every run wrote a new vault slot. Only the last reference reached the step history, and conversation deletion sweeps only what the history holds, so the other slots stayed in the vault until a GDPR sweep of the user. Instructions are now collected across all of the turn's actions, without duplicates, and then applied once each.
- **`dependency` survives unnamed rules and requires `reference`.** It called `getName().equals(reference)` on every rule in the set, so a single unnamed rule anywhere caused a NullPointerException, and the turn failed, whenever any dependency was evaluated. `Rule.equals` had the same problem. Both comparisons are now null-safe. A `dependency` without a `reference`, which could only fail, is now refused when the ruleset is read. Rule names stay optional, and duplicate names stay supported.
- **Matcher semantics are pinned.** New `@Nested Semantics` tables in `ActionMatcherTest` and `InputMatcherTest` run the matchers against a real conversation memory. They pin that a comma list is an ordered, adjacent sublist, plus `empty`, `lastStep` on the first step, `anyStep` and `never`. The existing tests stubbed every lookup and asserted SUCCESS only, so they kept passing with `indexOfSubList` replaced by `containsAll`.

### Design decisions

- `tagMatches` is refused rather than implemented. Agent tags would be a new feature with no model field, UI or storage behind them, and a value the client sends can already be matched with `contextmatcher`.
- Instructions are collected first and then applied, instead of being remembered as applied across actions. Each runs at its first selection: the turn's actions, then `setOnActions`. The only order change is that a repeat no longer runs again later in the turn. With `[{"actions":["*"], x="default"}, {"actions":["greet"], x="hello"}]`, a turn with the actions `[greet, other]` used to end with `x="default"`, because the `"*"` entry ran again for `other`. It now ends with `x="hello"`, the same as a turn with `greet` alone.

**Files:** [`OutputTemplateTask.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/templating/OutputTemplateTask.java), [`DeploymentContextCondition.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/rules/impl/conditions/DeploymentContextCondition.java), [`Dependency.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/rules/impl/conditions/Dependency.java), [`Rule.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/rules/impl/Rule.java), [`PropertySetterTask.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/properties/impl/PropertySetterTask.java), [`rules-editor.tsx`](../../ui/manager/src/components/editors/rules-editor.tsx), [`behavior-rules.md`](../behavior-rules.md), [`properties.md`](../properties.md)

## 🐛 fix(schedules): the poller's claim re-checks due-ness; leases judged by the database clock (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/schedule-claim-correctness`)

### What changed and why

Two ways the cluster-wide schedule claim let the same slot fire twice.

**A re-armed schedule could be claimed again.** A poll reads its batch once and
then claims the rows one by one. The claim was a compare-and-set on state alone
(`enabled` and `fireStatus`), with no `nextFire` term. If another instance
claimed, fired and re-armed a row before this instance's loop reached it, the
row was PENDING again with a future `nextFire`, the claim matched, and the slot
fired a second time. The second fire carried a new `fireId`, because the id is
keyed on the claim time, so nothing downstream could tell the two apart. A fast
rule-only turn and a batch of up to `eddi.schedule.poll-batch-size` (100) claim
round trips are enough for this to happen.

`IScheduleStore.tryClaim` now also requires `nextFire <= now`, the condition
`findDueSchedules` already reads. A manual fire (`POST /schedules/{id}/fire`,
`fire_schedule_now`) may fire before the due time, so it claims through the new
`tryClaimAnyTime`, which has the same conditions without the due check. The
existing method became the safe one, so any future batch caller gets the check
by default.

**A fast clock could steal a live lease.** `claimedAt` was written from the
claiming instance's clock and compared with `now - lease` from the reading
instance's clock. With the default 10-minute lease against a 5-minute fire
timeout plus 30 s grace, an instance whose clock ran more than 4.5 minutes ahead
took over claims whose fires were still running. Both stores now write
`claimedAt` with the database's clock and measure a lease's age against that
same clock: MongoDB through a pipeline update with `{$toLong: "$$NOW"}` and an
`$expr` comparison, PostgreSQL through
`(EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM CURRENT_TIMESTAMP) * 1000)::bigint`. `tryClaim` and
`findDueSchedules` take the lease as a `Duration` instead of a precomputed
expiry instant. The instance's own `now` still decides due-ness and retry
readiness and keys the `fireId`; skew there only shifts when a schedule fires
on that instance, it does not duplicate a fire.

**Files:**
[`IScheduleStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/schedule/IScheduleStore.java),
[`MongoScheduleStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/schedule/mongo/MongoScheduleStore.java),
[`PostgresScheduleStore.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/postgres/PostgresScheduleStore.java),
[`SchedulePollerService.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/runtime/internal/SchedulePollerService.java),
[`ScheduleConfiguration.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/schedule/model/ScheduleConfiguration.java)
(the `fireId` comment said "scheduleId + fireTime"; it is the claim time),
[`scheduling.md`](../scheduling.md#cluster-awareness).

### Design decisions

- **Change `tryClaim`, add `tryClaimAnyTime`**, rather than adding a poller-only
  due-checked claim next to the old one: the unsafe variant is the one that has
  to be asked for by name.
- **The same tests run on both backends.** The container-backed
  `PostgresScheduleStoreTest` and `datastore/mongo/MongoScheduleStoreTest` each
  gained "a re-armed row is not claimed again (but Fire now still claims it)"
  and "a fast clock does not steal a live lease"; both failed on `main`.
- **`CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` rather than `clock_timestamp()`** in PostgreSQL: the
  value is fixed for the statement, so the write and the comparison in one
  claim use the same instant, and it matches the existing
  `PostgresConnectionNameClaimStore`.

### Behaviour changes

- A schedule that another instance fired and re-armed between a poll's read and
  its claim is no longer fired a second time.
- Instances whose clocks disagree no longer take over each other's running
  fires. An existing CLAIMED row written by an older version carries an
  application-clock `claimedAt`; after the upgrade its lease is judged against
  the database clock, which differs from it only by the skew this fix exists for.
- MongoDB: a CLAIMED schedule with no `claimedAt` now counts as an expired
  lease and is reclaimed (recorded as a failed attempt and retried), where it
  used to stay CLAIMED and never fire. No claim produces such a row, but
  `createSchedule` stores the caller's `fireStatus` as given, so a REST create
  that sends `"fireStatus": "CLAIMED"` could. PostgreSQL always inserts
  PENDING, so it never holds one.

## 🔒 fix(secrets): bearer redaction in any case, fail-closed export scrub, legacy KEK removed, loud grant-check errors (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/secret-redaction`)

### What changed and why

- **`SecretRedactionFilter` redacts a bearer token whatever the scheme's case.** The shape rule matched only `Bearer` with a capital B, the explicit-scheme rule listed `Basic|Token|Digest` but not bearer, and the name-bound rules stop a value at the space after the scheme word, which at six characters is under their floor. So `authorization: bearer <token>` and `Authorization: BEARER <token>` went through `redact()` unchanged, and so did a canonical `Authorization: Bearer <token>` with a token under 20 characters. The bearer rule is now case-insensitive and starts at a word boundary (`forbearer …` is prose, as for the `sk-` rules), except that the second character of an escape does not count: in serialized text a line break before the scheme is `\n`, and the canonical rule, which had no boundary, matched there. Its replacement stays `Bearer <REDACTED>`, the spelling the already-redacted checks recognise, so a second pass changes nothing. The explicit `authorization:` rule now lists `bearer` too, with no length floor, like `Basic`. `redact()`'s Javadoc, orphaned above `redactCredentialShapes`, is back on `redact()`.
- **`SecretScrubber.scrubJson` fails closed.** It caught every exception and returned the input — credentials included — with a WARN. Serializer output can still fail to parse (Jackson's read constraints, such as the 1,000-level nesting limit, do not bind the writer), and a bug in the scrub walk failed open the same way. It now throws `IllegalStateException` on a parse or write failure, carrying neither the input nor Jackson's exception (whose message quotes source text), and lets any other exception propagate. `containsCredential` already failed closed on the same input. At the callers the export aborts, or stops writing the snippet or schedule section at that document. An agent sync's remote source skips the document (the sync fails when it is the agent itself), and `RemoteApiResourceSource` remembers the refusal like a failed read, so a document several steps name is not fetched again for each of them.
- **The legacy fixed-salt KEK path is gone from `EnvelopeCrypto`.** `deriveKeyFromString(String)` (deprecated since 6.0.2), its `LEGACY_SALT` and `sha256Hex` had no caller in main code: the provider derives every KEK with the salted overload, `VaultSaltManager` holds the only live copy of the legacy salt, and `VaultChecksum.legacy` is the production legacy checksum. The four tests that built legacy checksum fixtures use `VaultChecksum.legacy`. `EnvelopeCryptoTest` now runs its encryption tests on random DEKs (no PBKDF2) and covers the salted overload's salt-length guard, which nothing tested.
- **The deploy-time grant check says when it could not check.** `VaultGrantChecker.isGranted` treated every metadata-read exception as granted and logged it at DEBUG — right for a secret that does not exist yet, but identical for a vault-store outage or a corrupt row. The resolver checks no grants, so that agent kept access to a restricted secret until its next deploy, with no trace. A not-found secret and an unparseable reference stay quiet; any other failure now logs a WARN naming the secret and the agent. It is still let through, as the class contract chooses. Documented in [secrets-vault.md](../secrets-vault.md#what-counts-as-granted).

### Design decisions

- **No new counter for the skipped grant check.** A store failure is already counted by `eddi.vault.errors.count` inside `VaultSecretProvider.getMetadata`; the WARN line names which agent and secret went unchecked, which a counter cannot.
- **The preflight's `checked` stays `true` when a grant could not be read.** Reporting `false` needs the checker to return the count of unverifiable references alongside the violations, which changes the method every gate test mocks. Left as a follow-up; the page says so.
- **The scrubber's exception drops its cause.** A parse error's message quotes the token it stopped at, which can be the credential, and a cause is printed with the stack trace that logs the exception. The exception class name is kept.

**Files:** [`SecretRedactionFilter.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/secrets/sanitize/SecretRedactionFilter.java), [`SecretScrubber.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/secrets/sanitize/SecretScrubber.java), [`EnvelopeCrypto.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/secrets/crypto/EnvelopeCrypto.java), [`VaultGrantChecker.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/secrets/VaultGrantChecker.java), [`RemoteApiResourceSource.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/backup/impl/RemoteApiResourceSource.java), [`secrets-vault.md`](../secrets-vault.md), and their tests.

## 🐛 fix(tenancy,connections): agent quota outage pre-registered, authorize needs a per-user connection, UTC grant timestamps (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/tenancy-connections-small`)

### What changed and why

- **The first agent-count outage now fires `EddiQuotaStoreUnavailable`.** `TenantQuotaService` registers `eddi.tenant.quota.unavailable` at 0 for the default tenant so that Prometheus' `increase()` sees the first refusal. The list it registered held `conversation`, `api_call` and `cost` but not `agent`, which the deploy gate also records, so that series was born at 1 and the first agent-count outage, the case the pre-registration exists for, never alerted. `agent` is now on the list ([`TenantQuotaService`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/tenancy/TenantQuotaService.java)).
- **`POST /connections/{name}/authorize` refuses a connection that cannot take a per-user grant.** It checked only `authType == OAUTH2_AUTHORIZATION_CODE`, while the callback also requires `binding: PER_USER` and refuses before redeeming the code. Since 6.4.0 a write cannot store that combination, but validation runs only on the write path, so a connection saved earlier with the default `SERVICE` binding still loads: the user went through the provider's consent screen and came back to `?error=exchange_failed`. `authorize` now answers **400** naming the binding and the fix, and issues no state and no cookie ([`RestConnectionAuthorization`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/connections/rest/RestConnectionAuthorization.java), [`connections.md`](../connections.md#per-user-accounts)).
- **PostgreSQL grant and OAuth-state timestamps are UTC wall clocks.** The columns are `TIMESTAMP` without time zone. `refresh_lease_expires_at` was already written and read as UTC. `expires_at`, `created_at`, `updated_at` and `last_refresh_at` in `connection_grants`, and `created_at`, `expires_at` and `consumed_at` in `connection_oauth_states`, went through the JDBC driver's default, the JVM's zone, and the SQL stamps used `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`, which a `TIMESTAMP` column takes in the session's zone, also set from the JVM. Replicas in different zones therefore moved every access-token expiry by their offset (a refresh hours early, or a dead token served as live), and in a daylight-saving zone the repeated autumn hour could put a ten-minute OAuth state's expiry before its creation, so `claim()` refused a valid callback. Every bind and read now goes through the new [`PostgresTimestamps`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/datastore/postgres/PostgresTimestamps.java) with a UTC `Calendar`, and every SQL stamp is `(CURRENT_TIMESTAMP AT TIME ZONE 'UTC')`. The grant insert now names `created_at`, because an existing table keeps its old column default.
- **Hygiene.** `TurnCostMeter` takes `SpendSinks` as a constructor argument instead of a field that tests left null and the code null-checked; tests pass `SpendSinks.NONE`. `ResourceAccessGuard.callerIsAdmin()` duplicated `isAdmin()` and is removed; its two callers use `isAdmin()`.

### Upgrade note

On a PostgreSQL deployment whose JVM did **not** run in UTC (the container image does unless `TZ` is set), the existing grant rows hold local wall clocks. They are now read as UTC, so each grant's `expires_at` is off by the zone's offset until its next refresh rewrites it. East of UTC an expired access token can be presented for up to that many hours and the provider rejects it; unlinking and relinking the account clears it at once. West of UTC the token is refreshed early, which is harmless. OAuth states live ten minutes, so only a link in progress during the upgrade is affected, and the user starts it again. MongoDB stores instants and is not affected.

### Design decisions

- **A UTC `Calendar` rather than migrating the columns to `TIMESTAMPTZ`.** `TIMESTAMPTZ` is the cleaner type, but the change is an `ALTER COLUMN ... TYPE` in `createSchema()`, which rewrites the table under an exclusive lock on every replica that boots, and converts existing rows through whichever zone that replica runs in. The lease column had already settled on the UTC wall clock; one rule for the whole table is easier to keep right than two.
- **One helper, in `datastore/postgres`.** Other PostgreSQL stores write `TIMESTAMP` columns the same way. They are out of scope here; the helper is where they would go.

---

## 🐛 fix(openai): abandoned stateless streams end their conversation; mapping deletes match (2026-11-07)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/v1-bridge`)

### What changed and why

Three defects in the OpenAI-compatible `/v1` adapter, found in the whole-codebase review.

- **A stateless stream refused a concurrency permit left its conversation open.** `prepare()` starts a stateless turn's throwaway conversation before the response is returned, and only `bridge.stream()` ended it. The streaming body takes its own permit; when it could not get one, it wrote the in-band `429` error event and returned without calling `stream()`, so every such request under `max-concurrent-requests` load left an open conversation behind. The new `OpenAiConversationBridge.abandon(turn)` ends a stateless turn's conversation (a stateful turn's belongs to its chat and is left alone), and the refused body calls it before writing the error.
- **Mapping deletes were unconditional, so concurrent requests could split a chat.** When a chat's mapped conversation had ended, Open WebUI's completion and title requests both found the stale mapping; the slower one's `deleteUserConversation` removed the fresh mapping the faster one had just written, and the chat continued in a second conversation while the first stayed open and orphaned. Every mapping delete in the bridge now goes through `deleteUserConversationIfMatches` with the conversation the request found unusable — the same primitive Slack and the webhook handler already use — so the slower delete is a no-op, its create collides, and the existing insert-conflict re-read adopts the winner.
- **Model resolution probed access by exception, and a failed catalogue was cached.** `AgentModelResolver.mayUse` caught `requireAgentUseAccess`, which logs a WARN and builds a `ForbiddenException` per hidden agent; `GET /v1/models` is polled, so a deployment with many private agents filled the log on every listing. It now asks `ResourceAccessGuard.hasAccess(id, USE)`, which answers the same. Separately, a failed `getAllLatestAgents` produced an empty catalogue that Caffeine stored for `model-cache-seconds`, so one store error made every model unknown for 30 s; the loader now returns `null` on failure (not cached) and the call that saw it gets an empty view.

### Design decisions

- `abandon` is a bridge method rather than an `endConversation` call in the adapter: the bridge owns the stateless-turn lifecycle (`say` and `stream` end it in their `finally`), so the adapter never needs to know which turns own a throwaway conversation.
- The conversation-id response header and the prepare-time start are kept, so a stateless streamed turn still names its conversation.
- A body that never runs at all is **not** covered. RESTEasy Reactive skips every remaining cancellable handler once the connection closes (`ResteasyReactiveRequestContext.isHandlerCanceled`), and `ResponseWriterHandler`, which invokes the `StreamingOutput`, is cancellable. So a client that disconnects after `prepare()` has started a stateless conversation but before the response is written still leaves that conversation open, until the idle sweep (`maximumLifeTimeOfIdleConversationsInDays`) ends it. Closing that window needs a response-completion hook on the Vert.x response, which cannot be checked without a live server; it is left as a follow-up rather than guessed at.

### What's next

- End a stateless streamed turn's conversation when its response is disposed without the body having run (the disconnect window above).

**Files:** [`RestOpenAiAdapter.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/openai/RestOpenAiAdapter.java), [`OpenAiConversationBridge.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/openai/OpenAiConversationBridge.java), [`AgentModelResolver.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/openai/AgentModelResolver.java), [`open-webui-integration.md`](../open-webui-integration.md); tests in `RestOpenAiAdapterTest`, `OpenAiConversationBridgeTest`, `OpenAiBridgeSessionAndErrorTest`, `AgentModelResolverTest`.

## 🐛 fix(audit): admin-only endpoints under a data-plane root are recorded as administrative actions (2026-11-06)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/admin-action-audit`)

### What changed and why

`AdminActionAuditFilter` decided what is administrative by the first path segment alone, so every
mutating request under `/agents`, `/userconversationstore` and the other data-plane roots went
unrecorded — including the endpoints there that only an admin may call. Resetting a stuck
conversation's state (`PATCH /agents/{conversationId}/state`, `@RolesAllowed("eddi-admin")`) and
creating or deleting a user's managed-conversation mapping
(`POST`/`DELETE /userconversationstore/userconversations/{intent}/{userId}`, admin-only at class level)
left no record of who did it. The test that was meant to pin the exclusion listed three paths no
endpoint serves (`/userconversationstore/x/say`, `/a2a/agent1`, `/connections/abc/callback`).

The path rule stays, and one rule decided by the matched endpoint joins it: a mutating request on a
data-plane path is still administrative when the endpoint's effective `@RolesAllowed` does not admit
`eddi-user`. The roles are already declared on every endpoint, so a new admin-only endpoint under those
roots needs nothing remembered. The lookup follows Quarkus: a method's own `@RolesAllowed`,
`@PermitAll` or `@Authenticated` before its class's, the implementation before the interface that
declares the method (`ResourceInfo` returns the implementation method, and Java does not inherit
interface annotations — which is where EDDI declares its roles). The answer is cached per resource
method, so the reflection runs once per endpoint, not on every chat turn.

The data-plane test now uses real paths and a resource method that admits `eddi-user`, so it still
exercises the path rule.

**Files:** [`AdminActionAuditFilter.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/audit/rest/AdminActionAuditFilter.java),
[`AdminActionAuditFilterTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/audit/rest/AdminActionAuditFilterTest.java),
[`audit-ledger.md`](../audit-ledger.md#administrative-actions)

## 🐛 fix(audit): an erasure or export through MCP records who asked for it (2026-11-06)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/admin-action-audit`)

### What changed and why

The MCP tools `delete_user_data` and `export_user_data` call `GdprComplianceService` directly, so
`AdminActionAuditFilter` never saw them, and the service's own `GDPR_ERASURE` / `GDPR_EXPORT` event names
the subject's pseudonym but no actor. The ledger therefore showed that user X was erased, but not by
whom — while the same erasure through `DELETE /admin/gdpr/X` was recorded with the caller. Who erased a
user is the first reason the administrative-action log exists.

The record-building half of the filter is now `AdminActionRecorder` (`@ApplicationScoped`, constructor
injection, never throws, switched by the same `eddi.audit.admin-actions.enabled`). The filter delegates
to it, and the two MCP tools record every call through it in a `finally`: method `MCP`, path
`/mcp/<tool>/<pseudonym of the subject>`, the MCP caller as actor, and as status what the REST surface
answers for the same outcome — `200`, `207` (incomplete erasure or export), `400`, `403` (role refusal;
the `ForbiddenException` still propagates) or `500`. The subject is pseudonymised whole rather than
matched against path segments, so an id containing `/` cannot leave part of itself behind. The
recorder's and the filter's failure WARNs name the method and endpoint, no longer the request path or the
exception message (the filter logs the exception type only), which could hold the raw id the record
pseudonymises. A REST path parameter that contains `/` (sent as `%2F`) is now pseudonymised whole in the
recorded path too.

The entry tests moved from `AdminActionAuditFilterTest` to the new `AdminActionRecorderTest`; the filter
test now runs a real recorder over a mocked ledger and checks what reaches it.

**Files:** [`AdminActionRecorder.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/audit/rest/AdminActionRecorder.java),
[`AdminActionAuditFilter.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/audit/rest/AdminActionAuditFilter.java),
[`McpGdprTools.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpGdprTools.java),
[`AdminActionRecorderTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/audit/rest/AdminActionRecorderTest.java),
[`McpGdprToolsTest.java`](../../src/test/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/mcp/McpGdprToolsTest.java),
[`audit-ledger.md`](../audit-ledger.md#administrative-actions)

**Next:** the Manager's administrative-actions method filter offers `POST`/`PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` only, so
an MCP row is listed under "All methods" but not under a method; and a REST `GET /admin/gdpr/{userId}/export`
is a read, which the filter does not record, so a REST export still has no actor on record.

## 🐛 fix(llm): a model cascade keeps the agent's workflow and MCP tools (2026-11-06)

**Repo:** EDDI (`fix/cascade-workflow-tools`)

### What changed and why

A task whose tools come only from its workflow — an `httpcalls` or `mcpcalls` step, with the task's own
`tools`, `enableBuiltInTools` and `a2aAgents` unset (for example an agent built by `setup_agent` with
`mcpServerUrls` and built-in tooling off) — gets those tools on the single-model path, because the tool
loop offers everything its assembly finds. With a `modelCascade` it got none: the cascade decided tool
mode from `Task.isAgentMode()`, which sees only the task's own configuration, so every step ran as a
plain chat and the model answered without the tools it was written for. The same predicate made
`enableInAgentMode: false` not skip the cascade for such a task, sized the opt-in cost reservation at
one round instead of `1 + maxToolIterations`, and decided whether a spend refusal's fallback is shown.

- **One answer per turn.** `IAgentOrchestrator.offersTools(task, memory)` (new; `AgentOrchestrator`
  answers from `buildToolSetup`, the assembly the loop itself runs on) says whether the task has a tool
  other than the `answerMode: "tool"` reply tool. `LlmTask` computes `taskHasTools` once — the config check
  first, the orchestrator only when a cascade is configured or the reservation is on, so a plain chat
  agent pays nothing — and uses it for the cascade's tool decision (new `taskHasTools` argument on the
  `CascadingModelExecutor.execute` overload `LlmTask` calls), for `skipCascade`, for
  `ModelCallBound.phaseMaxUsd` (now takes `toolLoop`) and for a spend refusal's `usedToolMode` (which
  asks the orchestrator itself when the plan did not need to).
- **Config-time copy folded.** `CompatibilityRules` and `EmailAgentToolsInspector` carried the same
  "own tools or workflow httpcalls/mcpcalls" expression; it is now `Task.usesTools(hasHttpCalls,
  hasMcpCalls)`. `Task.isAgentMode()`'s Javadoc says what it is (the task's own tools) and points at
  both.

**Behaviour change.** An existing cascade on a task with workflow-only tools now runs the tool loop in
each step: its answer streams as one chunk, and `structured_output` confidence is downgraded to
`judge_model` / `heuristic`, as for any agent-mode cascade. With `enableInAgentMode: false` such a task
now takes the single-model path. A cost reservation for a workflow-tool task, or an `answerMode:
"tool"` task, now holds `1 + maxToolIterations` rounds.

**Files:** [`IAgentOrchestrator.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/IAgentOrchestrator.java),
[`AgentOrchestrator.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/AgentOrchestrator.java),
[`LlmTask.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/LlmTask.java),
[`CascadingModelExecutor.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/CascadingModelExecutor.java),
[`ModelCallBound.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/impl/ModelCallBound.java),
[`LlmConfiguration.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/modules/llm/model/LlmConfiguration.java),
[`CompatibilityRules.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/engine/compat/CompatibilityRules.java),
[`EmailAgentToolsInspector.java`](../../src/main/java/ai/labs/eddi/integrations/email/EmailAgentToolsInspector.java),
[`model-cascade.md`](../model-cascade.md#agent-mode).

### Design decisions

- **Ask the assembly, do not re-derive it.** Widening `isAgentMode()` (or a new config predicate) would
  have been a second copy of the discovery rules that cannot see attachments, LAZY placeholders or
  runtime MCP discovery, and would drift. `offersTools` is the loop's own answer.
- **Asked once, only when it matters.** The answer is computed in `LlmTask` and passed down, not asked
  again inside the cascade; a task with no cascade and no reservation never triggers the extra assembly.
- **The reply tool does not count** as a tool of the task: a cascade already runs the loop for
  `answerMode: "tool"` regardless of `enableInAgentMode`, and that rule stays.

### Tests

`LlmTaskCascadeWorkflowToolsTest` (real cascade inside `LlmTask`, mocked orchestrator: the loop runs
for workflow tools, `enableInAgentMode: false` skips the cascade, no tools keeps the legacy cascade, a
plain task never asks, the reservation holds four rounds, a spend refusal shows its fallback),
`AgentOrchestratorOffersToolsTest` (real assembly plus a stubbed workflow spec), `ModelCallBoundTest`
(round count follows `toolLoop`).
